My dear Mr. Bruabaugh:

forwarded to my office from Professor Wheeler P. Davey of
Pannsylvania State College.
The information submitted by Professor Davey is of impor-
894.92/10-645
tance to this Department, and I should be most happy to receive
such additional information concerning G. Asahara and Kotaro
Honda as he may have available. The names of Honda's staff and

Research" Tokyo dated 1929, would be most useful.
Any additional information concerning scientists and techni-
cians, and the fields in which they were most active, would be
most welcome to the Department if Professor Davey would be so
kind as to forward such information.

1. I inclose a copy of the letter from the British Embassy
to General McCoy on the matter of the British request for scientific
information in Japan. General McCoy requests that this letter be
Areated with the strictest confidence and desires me to point out
once more that the British interpretation of his remarks to Sir
George Sansom is not correct. What General McCoy said, in effect,
was thet the British should remember that the United States has
always kept them informed of all military information because of
the close cooperation which exists between the military intelli-
gence services of the two armies.
2. I would appreciate any suggestions which you can give
Mutilay

Dear General McCoy,
You will recollect that some weeks ago
you discussed with me the United Kingdom Government's
proposal that a Scientific Research Mission should be
sent to Jeren. On that occasion you informed me of
certain objections on the part of the United States
Government to such a Mission, adding that the United
States Government would be prepered to disclose to us
conficentially what information they had on Japanese
scientific resesrch, so far as its potentially
dangerous ap!lication was concerned. I duly reported
in thet sense to London.
Our original proposal for a Scientific
Mission was based upon a decision reached by Ministers,
and has not yet been withdrawn. But I should like now,
without any commitment as to withcrawal, to enquire of
you infcrmelly whether in the event of our withdrawing
the proposal, the United States Government would agree
1) to disclose to His Mejesty's Government
all available information on Japenese
scientific research, without qualification
as to its potential application
SECA
to accept in principle the right of the
Fer Eastern Comnission to send commissions
of enouiry to Japen to investigate any
matter from which it is not specifically
excluded by the terns of the Moscow
Agreement.
Yours very sincerely,
(s) George Sansom
Major General Frank R. McCoy,
2516 Massachusetts Avenue,
Washington, D.C.

=== Page 6 === (confidence 0.97)
June 5, 1946
MEMORANDUM TO COLONEL LAMBERT, FAR EASTERN COMMISSION
SUBJECT: British Request for Information.
I regret the delay in replying to Colonel Babcock's memorandum
of May 21, 1946, but unfortunately I was called out of town and have
just returned.
The two questions raised by Sir George Sansom in his Memorandum
of May 17 to General McCoy have been informally discussed in the
State Department.On the basis of these discussions, I have reached
the following coaclusions:
The dieclosure to the British Government of information on
its potential application, is a question for decision by
the Army and Navy.
894,92
Contral
b.
In principle there is no objection to the right of the Far
Eastern Commission to send missions of inquiry to Japan.
The Commission itself, by the Moscow Declaration, is
specifically granted the right to meet wherever it may
choose, including Tokyo, and it would therefore be incon-
sistent to prohibit a part of the Commission, viz., a
mission chosen by it, at such time and for such purposes
as would be agreeable to SCAP, to proceed to Japan. It
should, of course, be clearly understood that such a mission
would in any event report to and be under the authority of
SCAP, and that its activities would not go beyond the terms
of reference of the Commission as specified in the Moscow
Declaration. In practice we would, I assume, discourage
such missions.
E. A. GROSS
RM·R
Special Assistant
Anal
Sev

certain that requests from the United Hingdom military representative

and that there will be no necessity for action by the Far Bastern
Commission.

26th August, 194
Dear General Hilidring,
In Sir George Sansom's absence I am writing
to thank you for your letter of the 20th August about
scientific research in Japan.
I have re-read the correspondence on this
subject and have studied your letter curefully to see
whether it gives us the asis for a reply to the Foreign
Office. We are, as you know, under instructions to
seek an assurance from the United States Government that
they wculd make available to the United Kingdom Govern-
ment all available information on Japanese scientific
research, without qualific tion as to its potential ap-
plication. You were good enough to mention that the
Supreme Commander "is authorised to honour certain re-
quests for that class of inforu tion" but I cannot avoid
the conclusion that the Foreign Office would not regard
that as covering their expression "all available inform-
ation on Japanese scientific research, without qualifi-
cation as to its potential ap lic tion."
I wonder whether I may, in the circumstances,
ask you to consider wheth r an answer on this point could
be given in language identical with,or very similar to,
that used in the quotation from the'Foreign Office
instructions to us?
Sincerely yours,
H. A. Graves.
Major General J. H. Hilldring,
Assistant Secretary of State,
WASHINGTON, D. C.

SUBJECT:
British Request for Information on Japanese Scientific Research
(8.100 216/4.
Fursuant to informal agreement of Silloc members, the problem
presented by the British request for information on Japanese scientific
research was relerred to a special comiittee consisting of Mr. Rusk,
Captain Dennison, and myself for consideration and recommendation to
SWHCG.
As appears from the attached memorandum of conversation, a formula
has been found on the basis that the British and Anerican Intelligence
representatives would discuss specific items of information desired by
the British and that steps woulu be taken to obtain desired information
to the extent that such infornation was not already available. No
problem of atomic oner y or other war-connected research appears to be
involved. This procedure is acceptable to the British representative,
who, moreover, stated that it was not necessary to reply to his letter
to you dated August 26, 1946.
In line with the foregoing, no further Sricc action appears
necessary at the present time. in view of certain inaccuracies in the
memorandum, such as the statement that atomic onergy is probably the
most important type o! research in which the British are interested, and
in view ot certain  olitical implications in the memorandum which appear
to be irrelevant in the light of the information actuelly desired by the
British Goverrment, it is recomrended that your memorandum of Sept. 16,
ub
1346 (8.. 100 216/+) be w thdrawn ana the copies which nave been distributed
he recalled and destroyed.

MEMORANDUM
His Majesty's Government in the United Kingdom were
550
informed of the United States proposal, made during a dis-
cussion at the War Department on the Sth Novenber last, that
8
British scientists should participate in an American invest
gation into Japanese pure and applied scientific research.
In order that a suitable list of research enquiries might
prepared it was also suggested that the United States Head
of Military Intelligence should confer with the British
Senior Military Intelligence Officer.
2.
His Majesty's Governnent consider that the scientific
reports already received probably cover military requirements
0
adequately but they are of the opinion that much academic re-
H
search, especially on subjects not directly concerned with
6
waging war, must have remained untapped.
. In these circumstances
e
His Mejesty's Government think that a non-military approach to
Cu
the subject would yield more valuable results and they would
accordingly prefer to transpose the matter from a military to a
civil plane. Experience gained in Germany has shown that better
results are obtained from inforual discussions between individual
men of science than from enquiries nade on a different scientific
level.
The State Department may moreover agree that other non-
military missions to Japan, such as the United States Educational
jand Textile Missions, have proved successful both in the manner
of their findings.
His Majesty's Government are mindful of the fact that
any such civilian mission would have to be careful to avoid
repeating, or over-lapping with, the more technological enquiries
made hitherto and to confine itself to academic research. To
ensure/

=== Page 12 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
ensure that the Mission would be of the high calibre suitable
to these circuastances and to the nature of the task, His
Majesty's Government propose, for their part, to select two
senior academic scientists, one for the physical and one for
the biological sciences.
4
His Majesty's Government would be pleased to know
whether the United States Government is able to concur in the
formation of this joint scientific mission on a non-military
basis. If so it would be of great assistance if the views of
the United States Government on the date of departure of the
Mission and the duration of the investigation could be com-
municated.
5.
Having in mind the prevailing logistic difficulties
His Majesty's Government would have to rely on the help of
the United States authorities over housing, food and transport
but they trust that, as the British delegation would be small,
no undue strain would be placed on American facilities.
BRITISH EBASSY,
WASHITGTON, D. C.
11th March, 1947.

Dear Sir:
71.147
Shortly after the end of the war in the Pacific, regulations
were set up for control of research in Japan. This, I under-
stand, was to make sure that research would not be direeted
.92/6-1247
toward weapons of wer. Under this regulation, physicists
were allowed to continue using their cyclotrons and doing
other research in the field of nuclear physics. A littlo
later, the order was revoked and the cyclotrons were destreyed.
The outburst of protests from American scientists indicate the
reaction of scientists the world over to this senseless measure.
Since that time, research in nuclear physics in Japan has been
held up. Our scientists, who have studied the situation in
Japan, have repeatedly recommended that this restriction should
be withdrawn. Would you please tell us who is responsible for
the present order and how this organization and American
scientists as a whole can help to get a sensible policy adopted.
2
At present, it is not possible to send scientific periodicals
3
and information to Japanese scientists or for them to come to
this country to work. We would also like to know why this is
so, and what can be done to reestablish the interchange of men
and knowledge which is so important for world scientific
developments.
FILED
Sincerely yours,
w. a: diqinbothn
W. A. Higinbdtham
WAH/mlb

(ADO)

TO
ADO - Mr. Schuler
DATE: June 30, 1947
FROM :
IS - Mr. Hall
SUBJECT:
Letter from W. A. Higinbotham, Federation of American
Scientists, dated June 12, 1947.
In reference to the attached letter, I would suggest the
following course of action:
1. Responsibility for the present order effecting
scientific research in Japen does not rest in this Division.
I would suggest exploring this question with General Hilldring's
office.
FW
?. The question of sending scientific periodicals and
894.92/6-1247
information to Jepanese scientists is also not within the
jurisdiction of this Division. I again suggest General
Hilldring's office.
3. The question as to how scientists can help get a
policy adopted in reference to the study of nuclear physics
in Japan is related to the competence of this Division in the
sense that we are concerned with the international control of
atomic energy. The foliswing facts should be borne in mind:
Jepan is a defeated country and is presently under the control
of armies of occupation. This Government's policy in reference
to Japan. as well as Germany, should be constantly understood in
the light of the security requirements necessary in those
countries until setisfactory peace treaties have been developed
and accepted.
4. The continued support of this Government's program
for the internetional control of atomic enerey would be a
substantive contribution to the development of a "sensible"
policy in Japan end elsewhere. For one of the objectives of
United States proposals for the international control of
etomic energy is the encouregement of scientific research,
particularly in nuclear physics in such a wey that the
beneficial use of atomic energy will be maximized.
5. I trust that the suggestions above will allow you
to prepare an appropriste reply.
CS/JRC
Attachment - Ltr. dtd. 6/12/47
N1947

GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
SUPREME COMMANDER FOR THE ALLIED POVERS
Civil Information and Education Section
Media Analysis Division
PUBLICATIONS ANALYSIS, 21 March 1947
No. 96
The Japanese_Attitude Toward Science
Loss of the war and the resultant contact with American equip-
ment and technique have made thougntful Japanese increasingly min-
ful of and dissatisfied with the state of science in their own coun-
try and the abscnce of applied science in their irdustrial and daily
lives. Magazine writers decry their present Government's lack of
interest in science, the absence of facilities for encouragement of
scientific studics, and the failure of industry to apply technical
knowledge. No sense of inferiority is evident, and wiiters seem cnn-
vinced that the problem, if properly handled, can be overcome by the
Japanese themselves. The demand is for a speedy start.
The more visionary writers look into the future to expound on
the wonderful things that may be expected of atomic energy; but the
belief is clear that the first and greatest problem i; to create,
in the Japanese people themselves, an awareness of tre part that
science, properly utilized, can play in their lives.
Japanese Seiencc
Magazine writers are inclined to find the general level of Jap-
anese scicnce distressingly low and to deplore the lack of encour-
agement for scientists and for subsidization projests. Hachiro
Tago, writing in NYUSU, compares the money granted for aid to scient-
ific research in the budgets of the United States and Russia with
that allotted by Japan. "We feel that Japan overlooks something im-
portant. The bucget for scientifie rescarch is about 24 million
ven -- O.O4 percent of the total budget. * * * The fund for scienti-
ric research is always insufficient because of the rising prices of
commodities. Thus the activities of the Rikagaku Konkyujo (Physics
and Chemistry Research Institute) and all other scientific research
institutes and associations are very limited. The reconstruction
of more than 2o0 college buildings is very difficult, and the build-
ing of new establishments to replace demolished research institutes
is entirely impossible." The disappointing Diet appropriation for
scientific technology is said to give evidence that interest in the
welfare of the scientific group is still only perfunetory, and the
writer believes that an extensive rearrangement of the political and
social structure will be required before scientists can take their
rightful place and render proper service to the people.
A staff writer for DIAMONDo, commenting on an address by Dr. E.
A. Ackerman, technical advisor to the SCAP Natural Resources Se -
tion, speaks succinctly of the state of science in Japan. "The poli-
ticians of this country often mention the progress of science, but
this always remains a mere talking point.  They actually attach little
importance to the encouragement of science. Great accomplishpents are
achieved by a few scientists individually, but they represent excep-
tional cases and the general level of scienee is far below them.
1
-1-

=== Page 24 === (confidence 0.99)
And, as the general level of science is very low, those few who are
superior are held back. Furthermore, there is no connection between
theoretical science and proper application. This results from the
incomplete development of productive industry. The worst example
of this is in agriculture. * * *Scientific technique must be intro-
duced into agriculture on a large scale. General scientific educa-
tion is also necessary."
Another issue of DAIAMONDO carries an article entitled "Respect
Scholars and Give Engineers Better Treatment," by Kenkichi Ishiyama,
the magazine's editor. The great development of American science
is here cited as a leading factor in the American victory over Japan
and the writer tells of the world acclaim accorded prominent scient-
ists of other nations. On the other hand, Japanese inventors and
scientists are but poorly esteemed. How, asks Ishiyama, did Japan
reward Sakichi Toyoda, who invented an automatic weaving machine
used all over the worid, or Dr. Umetaro Suzuki, who discovered Vita-
min B and vas thereby responsible for the cure of countless numbers
of persons suffering from beri-beri? "Japan cannot reconstruct her-
self and prospor unlers she gives scholars and engineers better
treatment." The writer rofers not only to treatment by employer
or factory, but to the respect and esteem of the government and of
society.
Ishiyama follows up this article with another, in a later issue
of DAI!.OnDO, in which he laments that the native skill and ingen-
uity of the Japanese have not been extendec by application of scient
ific knowledge. During a trip in Aichi Prefecture he came upon an
amateur technician who had developed a new method of making cog
wheels, without knowing the scientific principles enbodied in his
discovery. An authoritative scientist, consulted by Ishiyama,
later confirmed his estimate of the merit of the process. Such
amateur technicians must be found and eneouraged and their works
must be evaluatd scientifically, says Ishiyama. Japanese methods
are still primitive. "The techniques ty vhich the Masamune sword
was forged and the Fambu kettle was manufactured, because of a lack
of scientific stud', remain unimproved." Japan's small manufact-
urers stick tenaciously to tried and true methods. "They depend
too much upon past experience, but if they should combine it with
science, their methods would surely become perfect. I believe that
this is the way tovard the rehabilitation of Japan!"
In an EKOl OMISUTO survey of the current state of Japanese in-
dustry by President Junzo Kurimoto of the Kurimoto Iron Works, there
is emphasis on the need of scientific preparation for the future.
"The encouraging policy adopted toward scientific technology during
the war vas nothing more than governmental ceremony, which included
no concretely beneficial mensures. It was the government policy to
accord kind treatment to scientific technicians, along with industri-
al war Workers, under the name Sangyo Senshi (Industrial Militia).
How are these technicians treated since the end of the war? So far
as I knov, there is no bucget for promoting scientific technology
this fiscal year; the Government has no interest along this line.
Look at the individual factories. How many factories are continuing
scientific study? In nddition, the salary of technicians is so low
that they can barely live. * * * The Asiatic regions will be rebuilt
in the near future by modernized industries under the scientific
leadership of England and America. Then Japan will fall far behind
in international industrial and economic competition and finally
will enter a dark age of culture. * * * Therefore, let me propose
that we establish a national institute to study and promote scien-
tific technology. * * *Labor unions should establish technical de-
partments to serve as intermediaries between research establishments
and the laborers. Technicians should be made conscious of their
mission in reconstructing industrial economy, readjusting enterprises,
and playing a leading role in the renovation of our country."

Scientific Thinking
Advanced scientific thought has been conspicuously absent from
the leading Japanese magazines, where emphasis is more given to the
popularization of science. An exception to this, however, is a
KAIz0 article by Hideki Yukawa, one of Japan's leading atomic physi-
cists, on "Scientific Thinking," which explains the methods utilized
for the advanccment of modern physics. Scientific thinking, Yukawa
declar s, is commonly understood to bc the process of reasoning
based on known facts. Such inductive logic is used to derive em-
pirical laws from many expericnces. Also necessary, says Yukawa,
is deductive logic; the mathematics which is the basis for advanced
physics is a systcm of deductive logic bosed on self-evident axioms.
To explnin the relation between empirical laus and to construct the-
oretical systems, scientists establish a hypothsis, deduce from it
many kinds of conclusions, and apply them to empirical laws to ver-
ify or disprove the thcory. Unsatisfactory hypothescs are abandoned,
and those which meet the test of all known experiences are called
fundamental truths. Around these fundamental truths, new and fur-
ther theoretical systems are v loped deductively. Newton's laws
and Einstein's theory of relativity are cited as cxamples of such
deductive proe: :scs.
In the sphore of theoretical physics at present, Yukowa ex-
plains, there re two great theories, the quantum theory and the
iden of rclativity. There is not yet sufficient harmony between
these two, and they fail to explain some phenomena associated with
cosmic rays and tho nucleus of the atom. A further and more com-
prehensive th oretical system is seen necessary by thc writer. "The
most important progress in scientific theorins is made by the im-
agination. Inductive logic and dcductive logic are not sufficient
in this circumstance. This is the most perplexing obstacle facing
our reseorchers; and it is commonly said that what is needed hcre is
the intuition of a genius." Yukawa concludes by declaring that
there is ? fundamental unity and rationality to all nature -- "no
matter how mysterious a thing seems, it can ultimately be understood
rationally."
In quite a differcnt vein is thc article by Ukichiro Nakatani,
Tokyo Imperial University science profes: or, in BUNGEI SHUNJU.
While Nakatani cmphasizes that he docs not disapprove the methods
used to acqunint younger children vith h bits of scientific and
rational behaviour, he feels that something of the child's natural
wonder toward the mysteries of naturc should be preserved. "One
of the important elements of science is to perccive the wonders of
nature in comron and familiar things." If men become too impatient
to teach factunl scientific knowledg to children, they may decrease
or destroy the child's interess in science. The necd for popular-
ization of scicnec -- which was necessary for the application of
scientific techniqurs during the war -- is said to have passed, and
Nakatani urges that children and persons yet unfamiliar with seience
be taught som thins of the love of nature and the wonder of nature.
Though science secks to explain all things rationally, Nakatani
writes, the mystery of life remains unconquered and hc quotes the
words of a Professor Torada: "The mystery of life is simply trans-
ferred to th mystery of atoms -- and the mystery of atoms remains
unsolvca."
The philosophy of a Icftist scientist, Kunio Oka, appears in an
article on "Religion and Science" in SHIN JOEN. Religion, Oka.says,
is an "cmbellishment" created by man to hide or rationalize class
distinctions and the oppression of the masses. Religion has been th
aggressor against scicnce, he asserts; the church, not wanting to
lose authority, has persecuted scientists. Sciencc docs not persist
in old theories, but values facts, and experience. Religion, de-
clares Oka, has no such courage or humility. "I do not seek the
absolute God," ho explains, "I try to attain the most beautiful hu-
man life through reason rather than through religion."

The Japanese Look at the Advance of Science
It is necessary for the Japanese to put science to daily practi-
cal use or Japan will be left far behind the other countries of
the world, writes Dr. Kyoji Yoshida, Waseda University Engineering
professor, in SEIKATSU BUNKA. "This is not merely a question that
affects the private lives of a few
v . . . but is a matter of rais-
ing the living standard of society." Although some rich people
may enjoy all modern hygienic improvements, truly advanced civili-
zation is not achieved if poor people living nearby have none of
these conveniences. "All people, rich and poor, must enjoy the con-
veniences of civilization," he maintains, by way of introducing a
plea for universal adoption of western improvements. "Science and
daily life have been two different matters. e have not applied
science to daily life." He then suggests such concrete ways of ut-
ilizing science as employing mechanical facilities in the home for
cooking, garbage disposal,, heating, ventilation and lighting. In
particular, he wishes to change the Japanese custom of hiding the
kitchen from the rest of the house, -- "opening up our kitchen"
and "opening up our life itself."
A lengthy consideration in ASAHI GRAPH depicts American progress.
"In Amcrica, where the people are proud of their scientific achiever
ments, not only experts, but even amateurs are absorbed in the study
of television."Almost as far removed from actual circumstances for
the average Japanese is the modern, well-equipped American kitchen.
The New Year's Edition of ASAI GRArH -- in a pictorial presenta-
tion of wishful New Year's dreams -- shows a poor housewife imagin-
ing herself in such a glorious setting.
A group of articles on "Science and Life" gives the feminine
readers of FUJIN ASAHI some idea how the principles of science may
be applied to their personal lives. Tai Tanabe explains that the
Japanese home, with a design now governed chiefly by economic fac-
tors nd simplicity in living arrangements, will be more scientifi-
cally planned and will include scientific devices, whdch he ex-
plains. Matsujiro Oyama writes about electricity, explaining
simple principles so that housewives will understand the purpose
of fuses and be able to prevent electrical accidents. He then dis-
cusses certain modern electric machines, but warns that Japanese
housewives will have to do without such things as refrigerators
and washing machines for : long time to come.
Explanations of the meaning of such terms as calory, nourish-
ment, and vitamins are included in Sadako Nakatani's article on food
preparation and scicntific diet planning. Such planning will enable
familics to gain maximum benefit from their food in these days of
shortage, she writes. Hideo Kumabe issues a plen for preventive
measures before illness is acquired rather than treatment later, and
says, "Hcnceforth the Japanese should leave their fate to heaven
only after all scintific precautions are taken."
Henlth and Hygicne
There seems to be inereasing awareness of Japan's poor status in
regard to public health and scientific sanitary facilitics. DAI-
AMonDo contains an article on penicillin by Yurei Nakano, describing
accomplishments of this drug which became world famous, the author
says, when it cured Winston Churchill of pneumonia in'two days.
American production methods are surveyed, and the vriter notes that
United States scientists already have progressed beyond penicillin
to find an even more effective medicine. Some Japanese companics
are producing penicillin in small amounts, but he fears that his
country never can hope to achieve the production accomplishment of
the United States. "Japanese society is not advanced. The level
is belov that of other nations. There is no understanding of science
There are no materials. There is no contributor of funds. People
makc a fuss only about law and literature. There is no progress.
Things are totally different in thc United States."

=== Page 27 === (confidence 0.99)
 The chances of abclishing disease from the earth are slim, Dr.
Tomio Ogata tells a staff member of SANDEI MAINICHI in an interview.
"It is possible to banish sickness caused by bacteria from some en-
tire districts and even some countries." Smallpox is given as an
illustration; it has been overcome almost completely in civilized
countries where vaccination is given. Typhoid and dysentery no.
longer occur in western countries, Ogata relates, but are found in
Japan quite frequently. Preventative injection for typhus is indi-
cated; malaria can be fought successfully with DDT. Fifty years
ago there were almost the same number of cases of tuberculosis in
America as in Japan; now the rate in the United States has been cut
tremendously while in Japan it remains about the same. There is a
preventative vaccination for this too, Ogata explains. But he is
not optimistic over prospects for advancing the treacment of arter-
iosclerosis and mental disorders, and concludes witn philosophical
statements to the effect that good health is onl: one of the factors
involved in a happy life.
Measures for combating winter cold are offereu in FUJIN GAHO by
Hiroshi Maeda, who believes that the most effective method of pro-
tection is the hardening of the body to withstand cold -- and that
this is best accom lished by sun bathing. He gives hints for insul-
ation of floors, windows, and walls; shows the relative effectivenes
of various types of fuel, and points out the importance of maintain-
ing proper ventilation. There are also suggestions concerning the
best types of clothing, and the advice that face masks should be
used only by persons suffering from colds. In a review of 1946,
ASAHI GRAPH stresses the lack of Japanese health and sanitation
facilities. A picture shows DDT being spread in a city street, and
the accompanying caption reads, in part: "There is nothing to boast
about in typhus and itches, which prevail all over Japan. There are
not enough doctors or medicine. There is no DDT of Japanese manu-
facture. There is no ward for the hospital patient. There is no
knowing who will catch diseases, for there is no conception of ay-
giene among the people.* * *There are no rats killed. There is no
cleaning out of the ditches. There is no notice to the authorities
when a person is diseased.* * *There is no halting of infecticr "
Atomic Power
Japanese interest in atomic power scems to steer clear of the
physical cffects of the atomic bomb and to center around hope and
expectation of development of this new source of cnergy for the good
of mankind. Kazuo Ishikawa, former mcmber of Kyushu Imperial Uni-
versity's department of medicine, titlcs his SHUKAN ASAHI article
"Atomic Power Calls for Happiness." He begins with an account of
the visit of the American Scientific Research Mission, headed by
Col. Stafford Warren, to the devastated city of Nagasaki. He then
quotes a note written by thrce American scientist friends of the
Japanese physicist, Dr. Ryokichi Sagane -- which was drcpped from th.
plane that carried the bomb. The letter urged Professor Sagane to
convince the Japanese people and their leaders that continuation of
the war could lead only to demolished cities and great loss of life,
and expressed the regret of the scientists that their achievement
had to be directed toward such an end. Ishikawa then declares that
the work of scicntists aims primarily at "the happiness of mankind,"
and discusses possible uscs of atomic power in the ficlds of medicin
industry, and agriculture, and in producing atmospheric phenomena,
A simply-written tract on atomic power by Taimei Yoshimura appear:
in MADEMOISELLE. This writer follows the lead of others in bewailin
Japan's lack of scientific development: "In th field of atomic pow.
er we have Dr Hidcki Yukawa and Dr Yoshio Nishina. But however
great, they are of little use when there are such a few. America
cannot have many scholars who are better than Dr Yukawa or Dr Nishin:
but thcre are hundreds who are as good as they are. And there are
thousands of Americans who are ready to master and apply the results

=== Page 28 === (confidence 0.99)
of their study, and millions of people equipped with ample knowledge
to understand and support it. That is why the atomic bomb was not
invented in Japan, but in America." Yoshimura develops an imagin-
ary picture of the application of atomic energy in civil engineer-
ing, mini'g, fishing, and other fields, and concludes with a fanci-
ful dream of a great atomic power company in which all the people
of the world cooperate, "discarding petty prejudices and jealousies
which has as its purpose "to supply abundant atomic power in order
to devclop civilization and the welfare of mankind."
Another interpreter of the wonders of atomic energy is Jun Nagai,
who writcs in NYusU. There are marvellous new inventions in the
world today, Nagai reports, including Professor Bogomolets' rejuven-
ating serum, a process of making cloth directly from cotton without
spinning, and a boiler which utilizes powdered rather than solid
coal. "The miraculous progress of science will, in the course of
time, prove greater than national boundaries and races, as it moves
ever forward."
ScientificAoplications
By applying imaginaticn and ingenuity, Japan may find much new
wealth to cultivate, a TOYO KEIZAI SHIMPO staff writer declares.
He maintains that there is much room for development of Japan's
natural wealth, and that it is man, not nature, that creates re-
sources. Thus coal or hydraulic pover were comparatively useless tc
mankind in the medieval period, but modern man, through study, has
made them an indispensible part of his daily life. Japan lacks the
type of iren ore possessed by other countries, but has large quan-
tities of limonite. She faces the problem, therefore, of finding
a new way to process this basic iron ore. Likewise, when advanced
and efficient methods of fishing are adopted, yield will be in-
creased and cost diminished. New industries can be established
when systematic methods of plonting and forestation are inaugurated.
"Needless to say," the author explains, "the industrialization of
new resources may encounter various difficulties. But . . . we
must start new peace-time industries as early as possible. Concur-
rently, we must increase the productivity of our mnnpower, of which
we have an abundance. For such purposes, it is absolutely neces-
sary to create an extensive research laboratory, through which the
systematic search for sources of new wealth must be carried out."
A SiUKAN ASAHI article by Minoru Iida and Ichiro Kinimoto de-
scribes the damage wrought by the recent earthquake and accompany-
ing tidal waves, and quotes a seismologist at Kyoto Imperial Uni-
versity as st ting that modern scientific equipment makes it pose
sible to predict earthquakes ccurately. "It is notable that the
damage caused by tidal waves, floods and fires following earthquake
is greater than that caused by the quake itself. * * * In the futur
effective measures to prevent damage from tidal waves must be incor-
porated into our city planning."
An engineer, iasanori liose, discourses in KINGU on the construc-
tion of larg-scale dams for generation of hydro-electric power.
With present coal supply inadequate and future supply uncertain,
"the consumntion of coal for generation of electricity has come to
be considered uneconomical. * * * The construction of a dam may
cause the loss of hundreds of houses and large amounts of fertile
land. * * * This is a serious problem, but the creation of the dam
has been proved to be profitable, warranting even the considerable
sacrifices which are inevitable." Nose explains that -- in addition
to furnisning power -- dams create sources of water, supply irriga-
tion systems, and provide flood control; and urges that because of
the great cost of construction and maintenance, the building and
operntion of dams should be a Government function.

=== Page 29 === (confidence 0.97)
 A visionary scientist, Takatsugu Hidaka, offers in SHUkAn ASAHI
the unusual idea of expanding the Bering Strait and diverting the
warm ocean current from the south into the Arctic Sen, thus pro-
ducing a mild climate on the north coast of Alaska. This proposal
(which Hidaka attributes originally to Steinmetz) is not so far-
fetched, he maintains, now that atomic power has been harnessed.
Hidaka also discusses several other ideas. "Although our territory
has been cut in half, we have a boundless ocean. * * * If we work
scientifically, we need not be disappointed at the narrow confines
of our country. * * * Should we fill up the Inland Sea, our land
would be greatly increased at a single stroke. * * * Raising the lev-
el of sciencc does not always cntail the emergence of great individ-
ual scientists, but sometimes an extension of the popularity of
science.
Conclusion
Through all these articles it is possible to see the Japancse
admiration for Am-rican scientific knowledge and application,
and the hope that Japan will be able to absorb some of this knowledge
and duplicato some of the ccomplishments. Examination of the fore-
going articles seems to rcveal a resolution among thinking Japanese
to strive for the advancemont of their country and its people
through scientific nchicvement.

SCIENTIFIC MISSION
It will be recollected that His Majesty's Government
advocated the adoption of a olicy in the Far Eastern Com-
mission regarding Scientific Research in Japan.
At the urgent recuest of the Chairman of the Commission
this  roposal was withdrawn by Sir George Sansom since it was
made clear to the Uaited Kin don Delegate that prosecution of
the latter wouid rove ombarraving to the United States
Government. At the same time General McCoy assured Sir George
Sansom (as we believe in the utmort good faith) that His
FU
in jesty's Government vould be iven, con identially, what
inform tion was aveilr le on all types of Japanese scientific
research.
So far as urely military uetters are concerned our
wishes have probably been mtecuately met, but in the field of
1
Japanese pure and aprlied seientific research there is a wide
sap. A diseussion was held at the War Department on the 8th
November 1946 on the type of information recuired by His
is jesty's covernment and it wan then sur ested that one or two
British scientists shoid artici ate in an Americn investi-
pation - such as en uiry to be nde under military auspices.
His  jestyIs Government consid orod that the tter should be
transferred from the ilitar to the civil  lane and they asked
for facilities to send two well uali ied British scientists to

interested FKC countries shall be sent to Japan.

GRANDSHL EOE DHEORNATION
There have bosn a mumber of requests over a peried of e year by varlese
technelegy. In SANACC 216/5 the Department of State submitted a program fes 
investigation of Japanese tochnology which contemplated sending a mission to Japes
including personnel of all interested Far Eastern Commission member coumtries. Im
SANACC 216/6 the Joint Chiefs of Staff indicated that the plan was deemed undesirable
from a military point of view for security reasons.
The Australian member of the Far Bastern Commission introduced a poliey
statement providing for right of access by representatives of governments on the
Far Eastern Commission. It became clear from discussions in the Far Eastern
Commission that the substance of the Australian paper had the support of all members
of the Commiasion except the U. S. member, to whom no instructions had been given.
In SANACC 216/7 the Department of State proposed an amendment or counter-proposal
to the Austrelian paper. This proposal was transmitted to SCAP and disoussed with
SCAP representatives by Army Department representatives in the course of the resent
trip to Japan by the Under Secretary of the Army. In C IN
SCAP indieated
approval of this proposal subjcot to certain stated conditions of implementation.
SCAP also suggested the desirability of obtaining a reciprocal agreement on the part
of other members of the Far Eastern Commission for such investigations in areas
under their occupation.
Due to diplomatic intervention by the Department of State, the Australian
representatives on the Steering Committee of the Far Eastern Commission was
restreined for a period of three weeks from pressing the matter to a vote in the
Committee, although he and other members expressed mounting impatience over the
SECRET
878

=== Page 59 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
2
U. S. Government's failure to present a position. The matter beeame ene of
urgency when he stated at the meeting of the Steering Committee on April 27 that
Ambassader Nakin would be foreed to make a formal protest upon instruotion of his
Government unless the U. S. could state its position at the meeting on Mey 4.
The Department of Stato was unable to accept SCAp's suggestion as to
reciprocity since the FEc lacks jurisdiction to adopt a policy imposing conditions
on any member country with respect to action within any occupied aree other than
Japan. Also, other occupying countries have a greater degree of legally inde-
pendent authority in other occupied areas than the U. S. possesses in Japan, while
on the other hand Far Eastern Commission countries have certain legal rights in the
Allied occupation of Japan irrespective of policies they pursue in other occupied
areas.
After exhaustive analysis of the problem, State and Army Departments agreed
that the U. S. member of the Far Eastern Commission could accept the Australian
proposal in modified form as shown in Appendix A, subject to the conditions set
out in Appendix A, Annex A, a statewent to be made by the U. S. Member. It was
felt, however, that in view of the inability of the Government to accept the
reciprocity suggestion made by SCAP, that SCAP's final approval should be obtained
for the whole proposal. This was done by telecon between the Department of the
Army and SCAP.
It is believed by the Department of the Army and by the Department of State
that SCAP's approval of the program, subject to the conditions of implementation,
removes the security problem noted by the Joint Chiefs of Staff in SANACC 216/6.
For this reason and because of the extreme urgency involved, the Department of
State transmitted the proposed policy statement, together with the attached
SECRET

=== Page 60 === (confidence 0.98)
SECRET
-3
statement of conditions to the U. S. member on the Far Eastern Commission for
use at the Steering Committee meeting on May 4 in connection with the Australiem
proposal. It was made clear that the U. S. member should defend the power of
SCAP to impose the conditions as stated. It was also pointed out that 3CAP
should be advised that these conditions have been brought to the attention of
the Far Eastern Commission.

Subject: Scientific and Technical Investigations

894.92/2-1248
In SANACC 216/5, the Dupartment of State submitted a
program for the investigation of Japanese technology which
contemplated sending a mission to Japan including personnel
of all Far Eastern Commission member countries.
In SANACC 216/6 the Joint Chiefs of Staff indicated that
the plan was deemed undesirable from a military point of view,
and suggested that the proposed mission should consist entirely
of U. S. personnel.
In the meantime, other nations in the Far Eastern Commission
have pressed for direct access by their representatives to
Japanese scientific and technical processes. Appendix "A" to
this memorandum is a proposal submitted to the Roparations Com-
mittee of FEC by its Australian member.
The substance of the Australian paper appears to have the
support of members of the Committee except the U. S. member,
who has not yet been instrueted.
Appendix "B" is a draft counterproposal to the Australian
paper. This Department is inclined to favor responding to the
CS/A
Australian paper either with the proposal contained in Appen-
dix "B" hereof, or with the plan suggested in SANACC 216/5.
ey, it is requested that the subcommittee for the Far
Rastern Commission give immediate consideration to SANACC 216/5,
216/6 and this memorandum, to consult with the appropriate aganey
of the Joint Chiefs of Staff, and to submit to the Committee an
appropriate recomendation.

=== Page 63 === (confidence 0.99)
APPENDIX "B"
Representatives in Japan of any Governments of members
of the United Nations should be permitted access to and the
right to take ∞pies of the details of non-military technical
and scientific processes of Japanese origin and omership
which were developed prior to December 31, 1945.
SCAP is directed to institute procedures to ensure the
following:
(1) That specific requests for inspection are
made to SCAP, and a record of such requests is made
available in Tokyo for the information of representatives
of all United Nations countries.
(2) That all technical and scientific information
gathered by any representative of a member of the United
Nations pursuant to the authority contained in this
directive shall be promptly and fully disclosed to SCAP
who will make it available to all interested members of
the United Nations.
(3) That the investigations of such representatives
are coordinated to avoid duplication and are conducted
in a manner which will ensure that current production in
Japan is not unduly impeded.

3. Ectablishment of the Publication Board and the authoriza-
ticn of operations in its behalf by the Cffice of Technical
Services, Department of Commerce, was effected through Executive
Crder 9568 and Executive Order 9604. The full text of the latter
is as follows:
"EXECUTIVE ORDER 9604,
Providing for the Release of Scientific Information
(Extension and Amendment of Executive Order No. 9568)
"By virtuc of the authority vested in me by the Consti-
tution and Statutes, as President of the United States and
Commander in Chief of the Arny and Navy, and in order to
provice for the rclcasc and dissemination of certain scien-
tific and industrial information heretofore or hereafter
obtained from the cnemy by any department or agency of this
Government, to the end that such information may be of
maximum benefit to the public, it is hereby ordered as
follows:
"1. It is the policy of this Government, subject to the
reguirements of naticnal military security, that there shall
be prompt, public, free and general dissemination of enemy
scientific and industrial information. The expression
'enemy scientific and industrial information', as used
herein, is defined to ccmprise all information concerning
scientific, industrial and technological processes, inven-
tions, methods, devices, improvements and advances heretofore
or hereafter obtained by any department or agency of this
Government in enemy countries regardless of its origin, or
in liberated areas, if such information is of encmy origin
or has been acquired or appropriated by the enemy.

APPENDIX "B"
DISCUSSION
1. The Basic Directive for Post-Surrender Military Govern-
ment
in Japan Proper (SWNCC 52/7) provides that SCAP shal1 protect
ell plants, equipment, patents and records of large Japanese
compenies and research associations as well as laboratories and
research institutes.
2. In SWicc 3o9, which has not yet been adopted, it is
recommended that SCAP take steps "to collect and publish techno-
logy from other sources in Japan 1.e. other than published Jap-
anese patent specifications7. This...might include any industri-
ally useful technology that existed in Japan during the war...
ScAP shall authorize and lend assistance to the imnediate publi-
cation of...technological data of value, through any appropriate
3. Executive Order 9604 provides that "it is the policy of
this Covernment...that there shall be prompt, public, free and
general dissemination of enemy scientific and industriel informa-
tion."
4. Congress appropriated $2c0gc00 to enable the Dopartment
of Commerce to send technicians to Japan to inspect and report
on Japenese technology. Representatives of the War Department
and of the Alien Property Custodian testified in behalf of the
requested appropriations before the Congressional Appropriations
Conunittee.
5. Under Executive Order 9e04, mentioned above, the Publica-
tions Board, an interdepartmental board of cabinet rank, is
directed to publish enemy technology.
6. The British Government has formally requested permission
to send investigators to Japen, and undoubtedly other members
of the Far Eastern Commission have & similar interest in this
regard.

=== Page 77 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
7. The results obtained from the investigations condusted
in Germany justify the adoption of a parallel policy for Japan.
In view of the highly specialized character of the investigations
in Germany it was realized that the Department of Commerce was
the only agency of this Government equipped to recruit a body of
highly trained scientists and technicians who were faniliar enough
with industrial techniques to prepare the type of reports which
would be of the greatest utility to industry. As is known,
technicians fron other Allied governments participated in the
investigations of German
industry.
This program in Germany has
resulted in the p iblic dissemination of a large body of valuable
technical information.
Until & similar policy is adopted for
Japan the world will be deprived of the opportunity to utilize
Japanese industrial technology.
8.
The above facts, when considered together, indicate that:
2. The Urited States Government is committed to a policy
of prompt public dissemination of Japanese technology;
b. Congress has decided that these obligations can be
met most efficnciously by authorizing the Department of
Commerce to send a small number ci highly skilled technical
investigators to Japan;
c. The Publications Board will give Japenese technologi-
cal information public dissemination in the United States;
d. Members of the Far Eastern Commission other than the
United States are interested in sending investigators to
Japan.
e. The success of the investigations of German techno-
logy justify a similar program for Japan.
9. There is not as vast a body of industrial information to
investigate in Japan as there was in Germany, but this does not
necessarily imply that the task in Japan is less complicated or
that specialists are not required. All that this difference does
imply is thet a proportionately smaller number of highly skilled
technicians will be required for the task.

=== Page 78 === (confidence 0.98)
CONFIDENTIAL
10. Any technicel investigators permitted by SCAP to enter
Japan should operate under SCAp's control.
11. A limited number of technical and scientific investi-
gators, probably not more than twenty-five, from ell interested
Far Eastern Commission governments should be permitted to partici-
pate in the investigation.
12. The functions of recruiting United States personnel and
of supervising their investigations in Japen should be entrusted
to Mr. John C. Green, Executive Secretary of the Publication Board
and also Director of the Office of Technical Services of the
Department of Commeree. The Publication Board should be designated
as the agency of the United States Governnent for the publication
of the information on Japanese technology.
13. Adequate guarantees should be given that no unilateral
action will be taker by any Far Eastern Commission government
which would result in giving the industry of that government an
advantage over the industries of other Allied nations.
2. This guarantee should be supported by a conmitment on
the pert of each technician sent to Japan that he will report
fully to SCAP on ell technical information obtained by him
and that he will not diselose any of this information to any
unauthorized person before SCAP makes it available for publi-
cation or release to Far Eastern Commission countries.
b. All technical information gathered in Japan should be
given public dissemination.
14. The urgeney attached to this investigation is not unre-
lated to the success of European rehabilitation since prompt dis-
semination of information on new processes, improvements and
techniques developed by the Jepenese might play a useful role in
modernizing certain segments of European industry.

ENCLOSURE
THE JOINT CHIEFS OF STAFF
Washington 25, D. C.
SM-9515
26 January 1948
ME.ORANDUM FOR THE STATE-ARMY-NAVY-AIR FORCE
COORDINATING COMMITTEE:
Subject:
Program for Investigation
of Japanese Technology.
The Joint Chiefs of Staff have reviewed a study prepared by
the Department of State entitled Program for the Investigation
of Japenece Technology (SANAcc 216/5). While recognizing the
esirability of carrying out the United States policy for the
dissemination of Japanese technology, the Joint Chiefs of Staff
would point out that the method suggested in the study of intro-
jucing foreign investigators into Japan would have serious secur-
ity implications in that, regardless of any pledge or agreement
to the contrary, these foreign agents operating as authorized
technical investigators could obtain for their respective govern-
ments special information outside the scope of Japanese technol-
o y and establish cortacts in Japan which would be inimical to
United States security ir.terests in the Far East.
Ir view of the present critical world situation, the Joint
Chiefs of Staff do not favor the adoption of this method at this
time. The adverse security implications of the proposed inves-
tigation of Japanese technology would be removed if United
States personnel, preferably individuals from the Department of
Commerce, under supervision of the Supreme Commander for the
Allied Powers were used to collect the technological information,
which could be screened and then disseminated through the Fubli-
cation Board to the interested Far Eastern Comnission governments.
For the Joint Chiefs of Staff:
Jan 26 1948
/s/ W. G. Lalor
4343
W. G. LALOR,
Captain, U.S.Navy,
Secretary.

At the suggestion of Mr. Butterworth I requested Mr. Stirling
to come in this morning to discuss the above subject. I referred
to the paper which had been submitted by the Australian Delegate
on the FEC some six months ago which would permit scientific
investigations in Japan by representatives of all the United
Nations. I said that the State Department was very sympathetic
with the desire of the Australians to have their paper promptly
acted upon but that there were certain security considerations
which I wished to call to his confidential attention. I explained
that there was real concern in SCAP and in the Department of the
Army over the possible security dangers involved in allowing
investigators of all countries into Japen even though their work
S
was supposed to be confined to non-military subjects. I further
explained that the United States obviously had no security fears
with respect to Australian investigators but that there were other
nations involved regarding the activities of which this Government
was not so sanguine. I stated that in an effort to do everything
possible to meet the Australian desires an urgent message had been
dispatched to SCAP suggesting the possibility of devising certain
administrative measures which would effectively safeguard security
matters and at the same time permit the Australians and other
8
legitimate investigators to carry on their desired activity.
Mr. Stirling was told that a reply from SCAP was expected within
a week or two and therefore it would be greatly appreciated if
DOR· ND Un

Australians know that every possible effort was being made to do

QUESTIONS AND ANSWERS REGARDING SC-280/6
With Beference to Enclosure B. Paragraph 4.
1. UESTION - Will each FEC country have a minimum quota of two?
ANSWER
- Yes, for purposes conforming to the proposed poliey,
and subject to the other administrative conditions
set forth in the accompanying Statement by the U. 8.
member.
2. QUESTION - Will technical experts of Far Eastern Commission countries
who are already in Japan, such as experts conneoted with
reparations and restitution teams, who might have access
to technical information, be included within the quota of
257
ANSWER
- Not while they continue on duty in such capacities as
those desoribed in the cuestion.
3. GUESTION - Are there already in Japan United States technical experts,
and, if so, how many?
ANSWER
- Yes, there are technical experts on SCAP staff performing
various occupation duties including the implementation of
FFC-065. There are none assigned in the capacity provided
for by the proposed policy.
4. UEsTION - Would there be any administrative difficulty in permitting
the several FEC countries to alternete their technical
experts, wit the result that the number in Japan at any
time  ould never be over 25, although the totel for the
nine months would be over that figure?
ANSTER
- SCAP's purposes in setting the quota of 25 are to minimise
the administrative problems of implementating the proposed
policy and to minimize interference with current industrial
production in Japan. Any advance notice of specific re-
quests which may be contemplated by member countries of
the FEC would assist in the scheduling of the desired
inspections. SCAP could consider requests for the alterna-
tion of technical representatives on their merits, upon an
adequate showing of necessity, after the total quota of
25 for all member countries is filled.
With Reference to the Terminal Date of March 31. 1949.
5. UESTiON - Since this terminal date is stated to have no especial
significance, would the United States Government have any
objection to an extension of it?

=== Page 85 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
ANSWER - SCAP desires the completion of the proposed program in
the period specified in order to minimise interference
with Japanese production, a position which the U. 8.
Government fully supports. A specific request for
extension might be considered by FEC on fts merits in
the light of experience to be gained during the stated
period, and with reference to the progress of related
occupation programs.
With Reference to Enclosure B. Paragraph 3.
6. QUESTION - May technical experts in Japan, not members of missions

eycept as set forth in answer to luestion 8 below.

technical information, then would it not be advisable to

ANSWER - The support by the U. S. Government for the proposed poliey

MEMORANDUM FOR THE UNITED STATES MEMBER,
FAR EASTERN COMMISSION
Subject: Access to Japanese Technical and Scientific
Information in Japan
At the meeting of the Steerins Committee on June 1, the
member for Nen Zealand asked why SCap was not willing to allow
technical representatives of missions already in Japan to obtain
information, access to which would be provided upon adoption of
the proposed policy paper (sC 280/6). Upon being asked what
inquiry night be made to SCAP on this subject, the member for
New Zealand repliea, "With reference to question 2, (SC 280/7)
why not?"
The question posed by the member for New Zealand is con-
sidered unsuitable in form for transmittal to the Supreme Commander
for the Allied Fowers. As to its substance, it is evident from
previous communications that SCAP is unwilling to give blanket
authority to any and all technicians to have access to whatever
information they may cesire. The United States Government is
therefore of the opinion that no further inguiry should be made
of SCAF on this point.
If pressed further by the member for New Zesland, you may
reply that you are advised that SCAf does not wish to modify fur-
ther at this time his administrative arrangements as stated in
SC 280/6 and interpreted in SC 280/7, and that the United States
Government is of the opinion that no useful purpose would be
served by aduressing a further question to SCAP on this point.
You mey elso suggest that the positive resulte desired by the
members would be more expeditiously achieved by early action on
the FEC policy peper than by extended consideration of SCAP's
administrative arrangements.

=== Page 89 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
If pressed further by the Soviet member on the point as to
why new clearance of technical representatives already in Japan
should be necessary, you may point out that the activities result-
ing from the proposed policy constitute a new program whieh will
place an additional administrative burden on SCap's limited staff
and,unless carried out in an orderly fashion could interfere unduly
with SCAP's effort to increase Japanese production in order to
reduce the expense of occupation. SCAP, therefore, deems it meces-
sary to set up uniform arrangements for this program inoluding elear-
ance of all persons designated by member countries.
You should emphasize that insistence on further information ean
only delay action on the policy under consideration and urge that
the Com ittee members decide whether or not they wish adoption of a
policy on this subject or whether they wish to postpone aotion by
prolonged debate on detailed administrative matters.
Charles E. Saltsman
Assistant Secretary
