Extension of Far Eastern Commission Policies on
ACCESS TO JAPANESE TECHNICAL AND SCIENTIFIC INFORMATION IN JAPAN
The Far Eastern Commission decides as a matter of policy
that the provisions of the FEC policy decisions entitled "Aocess to
Japanese Technical and Scientific Information in Japan" (FEC-290/9
approved 24 June 1948)and "Access to Japanese Technical and Scientific
Information by Non-FEC Countries at War with Japan" (FEC-315/7. approved
23 December 1948) are hereby extended until 1 July 1949.
P-58
FEC-280/15
31 March 1949

=== Page 216 === (confidence 0.99)
( Exoorpt from Verbatim Record of 147th Meeting of th•
Far Eastern Commission, 31 Mareh, 1949 )
ITEM 2 - ACCESS TO JAPANESE TECHNICAL AND SCIENTIFIC INFORMATION
IN JAPAN (FEC-280/14, FEC-280/9, FEC-315/7)
GEN. MCCOY: You remember that this paper was considered last
week in connection with the time limit on the existing policy, which
expires on this date. There was an expression on the part of mem-
bers at that meeting concerning an extension of the period to the
end of the year. This was expressed by the Indian representative
and the representative from Holland. The United States suggested,
in view of this situation, an amendment to extend the period to the
lst of July, without prejudice to further extension after my Govern-
ment had received the opinions and the expression of the desires of
the other members of the Commission. I think at the last meeting
that the amendment proposed by the United States extending the date
to the lst of July was not acceptable, and I would like to put the
matter before you once more in view of the consultation by my Gov-
ernment with SCAP on the wishes expressed here and the fact that the
deadline date is now upon us. I was wondering if the members of
the Commission would accept what I can offer today, namely, an
extension to the lst of July without prejudice to further consider-
ation of your wishes, which are now before General MacArthur.
Would that be agreeable to you as representing your
Government?
MR. BANEFJI: Mr. Chairman, I take it therefore that the
views that we had occasion to express at the last meeting have been
considered by your Government, but that your Government is not in
the position to state a final position in the matter? I under-
stand the views that we expressed are still under consideration and
probably you want to have some time before you can express the
final view of your Government.
If that is so, Mr. Chairman, I must express my keen
disappointment that it has not been possible for you to take a
definite position on the views that we have had occasion to express
at the last meeting.
Mr. Chairman, you will recall you stated that for admin-
istrative and other reasons considered by the Supreme Commander to
be essential you were unable to approve the extension of the policy
as sugested, that is, to the 31st of December 1949. You were kind
enough to pass on the information which was furnished to your Govern-
ment by the Supreme Commander, that the countries concerned, that
is, countries represented here, have not been able to utilize fully
the facilities that have been afforded by this paper. I had
occasicn to point out at that time that that was entirely due, in
my way

=== Page 217 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 -
my way of thinking, to the inability of the Governments, within the
short period the policy was in operation, to make plans, to assemble
the necessary technical personnel, to arrange for clearance and
transportation, and for those persons to complete their investigations
within that period. I have now received further information from
India and I can assure you, Mr. Chairman, that our plans are now well
underway, and I believe also underway in other countries, as the
Netherlands member had occasion to point out at the last meeting, and
I am confident that if the extension is allowed up to the time desired
no opportunity will be lost by any member here to utilize fully the
time given by this paper.
Mr. Chairman, as you have indicated, we are facing the
deadline today. I believe that we should take some definite action
this morning for the very simple reason that it is preferable to
have the continuity of the policy kept and not broken by a lack of
action today. Prompted by this motive, and realizing, though
deploring, your present inability to approve the proposal as recom-
mended by the Steering Committee, I would reluctantly accept your
suggestion and approve the extension for the present up to the 1st
of July 1949. But I do so with the sincere feeling of regret and
I must make it perfectly clear that I accept this because of the
peculiar circumstance of the case and because of the deadline to
which you have referred. As you indicate, I want to make it a
matter of record that we are accepting this under protest and without
any prejudice to the question of further extension being considered
later on. And furthermore, Mr. Chairman, if it pleases the Com-
mission, I would suggest that the Commission keep on the agenda
the question of further extension, that is to say, we may have this
question on the agenda for consideration at the next meeting or
the meeting after that. That is all, Mr. Chairman, at present.
MR. REUCHLIN: Mr. Chairman, I can associate myself fully
with what Mr. Banerji just said. As today we have reached a
deadline we have to take a decision, and at this moment it seems
possible only to extend the period to the lst of July. This date
is certainly not the date which we wanted for our experts to go to
Japan and we want, of course, to maintain the date of the 3lst of
December.
I think it is up to us to judge how we have used our
time and it is not for the Supreme Commander to express any opinion
as to how we should have used it. When your proposal comes up
for a vote, I will not vote for it. I will abstain. I see no
other way out at the present moment. But I wish that at some
time in

=== Page 218 === (confidence 0.98)
P
-3
time in Tokyo, the Supreme Commander and his advisers would bear in
mind that they are the Allied Powers' representatives, and that,
therefore, when representatives around this table express interest
and have certain desires, those desires would be met. I would es-
pecially wish it in a case like this where we have had only a couple
of months to send experts and the United States Government has had
the opportunity of having their experts since the beginning of the
occupation either in uniform or as civil administrators. Therefore,
when we ask for an extension of six months or until the 3lst of
December, which is a very reasonable desire, I don't think we ought
to be answered that we have used our time insufficiently and that
we only can get another few months. I think it is a very reason-
able desire that we should want until the 3lst of December. It
has been weighed —the pros and cons - in our own official circles.
and we definitely must maintain our wish to have permission to go
to Japan for our experts until the 3lst of December.
GENERAL ROMULO: Mr. Chairman, the Philippines reiterates
the statement that I made on behalf of the Philippine delegation
at the last meeting on this question. We favor extension up to
December 3lst. Our representative in Tokyo has asked for that.
He has explained that our representatives — the two allowed us by
the Supreme Commander — did not have all the time they needed, so
he asks that I make representations in this Commission that the
period be extended. For this reason, I reiterate the statement
that I made at the last meeting. We are in favor of extension
up to December 3lst. Therefore, if this question is put to a
vote today, the Philippines will abstain.
MR. MAKIN: Mr. Chairman, on behalf of Australia, I wish to
associate myself with the remarks that have been made this morning.
We also feel strongly that the time should be extended until the
end of the year and feel that sufficient time has not been given
for our experts to have the opportunity of undertaking the duties
that they would wish under the provisions of this paper. We would
vote this morning in favor of the paper but reserve the right to
have this matter raised again so that there can be an extension of
the period until the end of the year. I fully agree with the
expression that have been made here this morning, and I hope that
the United States will be able to see its way clear to agree with
the proposal that have been made so that the time can be extended
until the end of the year.
GEN. ROMULO: Mr. Chairman, may I ask if a vote is taken
on this

=== Page 219 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
on this question today the matter of the extension of time be kept
on the agenda.
GEN. MCCOY: I am conecious of your wishes and will contimue
to try to meet them. I hope shortly to receive information which
we can put before you again on further extension. We are able to
make no commitment for the moment beyond that to extend the time
until the lst of July.
MR. BANEFJI: Yes, Mr. Chairman.
GEN. MCCOY: But I am hopeful that I can raise this question
shortly in a way that will be satisfactory to all of you.
Dr. Blakeslee calls my attention to the fact that there
was a suggestion that the paper remain on the agenda until we have
final action on it. This is what might be called a provisional
action to extend the period until the lst of July, and I'm quite
agreeable to that.
MR. BANERJI: That is what I suggested, Mr. Chairman: that
the question should remain on the agenda.
GEN. MCCOY: Is there any other comment, gentlemen, on this
temporary or provisional extension?
MR. FORD: Mr. Chairman, I have noted your statement that
further consideration of a further extension will not be prejudiced.
GEN. MCCOY: No.
MR. FORD: And in view of that important fact, and in view also
of the imminent expiry of the present policy, I would be prepared to
vote in favor of the proposal.
GEN. MCCOY: Well I would assume that there is no reason for
a formal vote on this provisional extension as long as the paper
continues on the agenda. If there are no objections, it will
accepted and the deadline of today go to the lst of July. The
paper will remain on the agenda for future action.

prejudice to further action which the Commission might desire to take to extend the
period to December 31, 1949.

a serious character as to justify veto of a paper extending the period to December

The Terms of Reference of the Far Eastern Commission provide
that one of the functions of the Commission should be to "formulate
the policies, principles and standards in conformity with which
the fulfillment by Japan of its obli ations under the Terms of
Surrender may be accomplished."
It is further provided that when such decisions are made by
694.92/4-749
the Far Eastern Commission, "The United States Government shall
prepare directives in accordance with the policy decisions of the
Commission and shall transmit them to the Supreme Commander through
the appropriate United States Government agency."
The Far Eastern Commission at its one hundred and forty-eighth
meeting held at 2516 lassachusetts Avenue, Northwest, Washington,
D. C., on 7 April 19l49, unanimously approved the enclosed policy de-
cision entitled "Extension of Far Eastern Commission Policies on
YR894.60
As Secretary General of the Far Eastern Commission, I have
been instructed to forward this decision to you on behalf of the
Commission in order that an appropriate directive may be prepared
and transmitted to the Supreme Commander in accordance with the
Terms of Reference.
The Commission agreed thet the text of the enclosed policy
should be released to the press.

Tokyo, on the subject "Inquiry of

(COPY)
GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
SUPREME COMMANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
Diplomatic Section
The Diplomatic Section of General Headquarters, Supreme
Commander for the Allied Powers, presents its compliments to the
Norwegian Diplomatic Mission in Japan, and has the honor to re-
fer to a communication, a copy of which is enclosed, recently
received from the Department of State regarding the availability
of certain technical information in Japan. In this connection,
the Diplomatic Section has been informed by the Scientific and
Technical Division, Economic and Scientific Section, General
Headquarters, that information on file with that Division is
available to the Norwegian Diplomatic Mission pursuant to the
provisions of a policy statement (FEC 315/7) adopted by the
Far Eastern Commission on December 23, 1948.
Information at present available covers the following in-
dustries: light metals, rayon and synthetic fibers, cotton
textiles, sericulture, tungsten carbide, shoe eyelet, rice,
phosphates, cement, tea, ramie fiber, ceramics, synthetic ferti-
lizers, bamboo handicraft, agar-agar, butanol fermentation, and
fuel gases.
The Norwegian Diplomatic Mission may wish to take up this
matter directly witi: the Scientific and Technical Division,
Room 322, Empire House, Tokyo, where the information in question
is filed.
Enclosure:
Department of State's Airgram
of April 19, 1949.
Tokyo, May 20, 1949

Dear Mr. Kennan:
1FC
May I take this opnortunity to extend you greetings from Japan.
I, Roy Hoko Ikeda, am now in Janan, where I arrived on April 15,
1949. Perhaps, you may be able to find out my past records easily
at the Federal Bureau of Investigations or Army InteRigence Office.
My purrose of coming to Ja an is to inform the Japanese public on
the real world situation and to promote goodwill among nations of
the world. For this specific puruose, I came to Japan to establish
a scientific research institute for the presentation of unbiased
facts.
Since my arrival here, I  as fully convinced that it is necessary
to set up a counter+research institution against the communist net
works, because I found most of the similar research groups in Tokyo
are communistic organizations or communist infiltrated organizatinns
although they seem to be conservative. They are smoothly functi
into various organisation for +he communistic aims.
I am  eking an effort to establish a contact with prominent fi
in Tokko mostly, of whom names and professions are shown on the
attached sheet. Incidentaly, I learned yest rday that a repres
tative of the Carnagie Endo ment for International F ace is her
I am very anxious to meet him, because it is my sincere wish to
obtain some subsidiary fund from that organization. If you ar
kind enough to make an arrangement for metwithin your power an
authority ppior to the denarture of him, I would appreciate ve
much.
My past research materials totali & about tvelve(l2) cubic me
tons are in my home at 152 North El Dorado Street, San Mateo, Calif.
If I were granted some fund from the Carnagie Endoment Foundation,
I wish to bring my v luable me terials to Janan in order to commenee
the research works promptly.
Your particular attention for my work out here is greatly appreciated.
The forthcoming announcement of the United States new policies for
the Far East under Mr. Jessup is anxiously awaited. Any aid you
can extend to me in my project to check communistic activities
in Janan and to democratize the mation is deeply appreciated.

The secretary of State forwards for the information
letter from Mr. Roy Hoko Ikeda with regard to his efforts
to establish & research institute in Japan. The following
information concerning Mr. Ikeda, which has been obtained
from the Department's files, may be of assistance in con-
sideration of hisletter.
Mr. Ikeda first made applicationg for an exit permit
to proceed to Japan in  letter dated October 10, 1946
to the Department. He stated that his specific purpose
in proceeding tφ Tolcyo, Japan, was to "join political
movement in Japan with cooperation of the Oceupation
authorities and to establish the Institution of International
The Visa Division on March 17, 1947 and the Division
of Foreign Activity Correlation on April 7, 1947 recommended
that his application be denied. This recommendation was
based on information received from a confidential source
within the Goyernment which reported that according to in-
formation received in 1943 the subject, who was formerly
known as a radical Communist among the West Coast Japanese
and opposed to the militaristic regime, may have changed
nis state to that of an extreme pro- Japanese nationalist.
Investigation at the Central Utah War Relocation Center
at Topaz, Utah, and Salt Lake City reflected that he was
known for his extreme liberalistic view points and was
considered a$ being violently opposed to the militaristic
regime then in control of Japan. Nr. Ikeda and his wife
left the Center in the latter part of August 1943 on in-
definite leave for the purpose of employment as janitor
and nurse's aide, respectively, at the Daughters of 7ion
Nursery, 140l North California Avenue, Chicago, Illinois,
and resided at 1528 North Fairrield Avenue, Chicago. He

=== Page 245 === (confidence 0.97)

received the People's World, Foreign Policy Bulletin,
New Republic, and PM. He was reported to have assisted
In the publication of a radical Communist newspaper on
the West Coast prior to his internment. He onoe sub-
mitted to Dr. Kenneth Colgrove, Professor of Political
Science, Northwestern University, Evanston, Illinois, a
plan for political reconstruction of Japan which calls
ror the creation of sufficient revolutionary feeling
to overthrow the Japanese Government of that time and
establish a new element who truly believe in the demo-
cratic ideal.
Mr. Ikeda was intervlewed in August 1944 and fur-
nished the following information:
He published War of the Classes and a magazine
Public Opinion in California and at the time of this
publication he stated that he was what would be con-
sidered a leftist and joined the Communist Party which
helped the circulation of his publications. He stated
that he remained a member of this party for approximitely
four years when he withdrew because he objected to the
"absolutism" of their doctrines. He stated that he
thoroughly believed in the political philosophy of Karl
Marx but he did not feel it was followed by the Communist
Party.
Mr. Ikeda stated that in 1929 he returned to Japan
where he affiliate himself with the Socialist Peoples
Party of Japan of which Dr. Ikuo Oyama was one of the
leading members. He advised that he actively engaged
in the work of spreading the doctrines of this party's
political ideology in Japan, but that in about i93l be-
cause of the ascension to power of the war cabinet in
Japan, Dr. Oyama was compelled to flee from the Japanese
empire. He stated that he was charged by the Japanese
Socialist Peoples Party with the responsibility of looking
after Dr. Oyama because it was hoped by the members of
this party that upon the ultimate fall of the cabinet and
the war party in Japan, the Socialist Peoples Party might
come into power and if this was the case Dr. Oyama was
looked upon as the most logical man to be the leader of this
new government.
Mr. Ikeda advised that he came back to the United
States in 1932, ahead of Dr. Oyama. He stated that since
his arrival in Chicago he had not affiliated himself with
any group, either of his own race or of any leftist political
movement in the United States. He stated that he had two
friends at the University of Chicago who could verify the
information that he was giving. These men he named as
Joe Koide and Ryoichi Fujii.

Detachment, SCAP, Tokyo.
